Classifying Topoi in Synthetic Guarded Domain Theory Journal: Electronic Notes in Theoretical Informatics and Computer Science Volume: 1
Daniele Palombi dpl, Jonathan Sterling jms

Abstract
Several different topoi have played an important role in the development and applications of synthetic guarded domain theory (SGDT), a new kind of synthetic domain theory that abstracts the concept of guarded recursion frequently employed in the semantics of programming languages. In order to unify the accounts of guarded recursion and coinduction, several authors have enriched SGDT with multiple “clocks” parameterizing different time-streams, leading to more complex and difficult to understand topos models. Until now these topoi have been understood very concretely qua categories of presheaves, and the logico-geometrical question of what theories these topoi classify has remained open. We show that several important topos models of SGDT classify very simple geometric theories, and that the passage to various forms of multi-clock guarded recursion can be rephrased more compositionally in terms of the lower bagtopos construction of Vickers and variations thereon due to Johnstone. We contribute to the consolidation of SGDT by isolating the universal property of multi-clock guarded recursion as a modular construction that applies to any topos model of single-clock guarded recursion.



The Universal Property of Multi-clock Guarded Recursion

keywords

Classifying topos, synthetic domain theory, guarded domain theory

1
Introduction

1.1
Synthetic guarded domain theory

Beginning with Scott and Strachey’s groundbreaking investigations in 1969, the
scientific study of programming semantics has been guided by the search for a
topology of computation — embodied in monoidal closed categories of spaces
called
domains
whose points can be thought of as the values of datatypes
and computer
programs
[
47
,
48
,
49
,
50
]
. The thesis
of denotational semantics under Scott and Strachey is that the computational
behavior of expressions in a programming language can be studied by
characterizing what values they take when interpreted as continuous functions
between domains; the advantage of denotational semantics over the
direct/operational study of program behavior is that, unlike the latter, it is
compositional and amenable to mathematical methods of reduction and
abstraction.

The need to reason about increasingly complex programming languages has drawn
researchers toward alternative theories of domains based on (complete, bounded,
ultra-)
metric
spaces

[
37
,
1
,
46
,
17
,
8
]
.
Metric domain theory has proved instrumental in untangling the circularities of
programming language semantics involving higher-order
store
[
9
]
; categories of metric spaces come
equipped with a pointed endofunctor
\frac{1}{2}\cdot-
to
scale
a given
space by half, which in combination with Banach’s fixed point theorem can be
used to prove algebraic compactness relative to mixed-variance endofunctors
whose recursive variables are guarded by
\frac{1}{2}\cdot-
.
From this one obtains a relatively simple interpretation of both recursive types and programs.

Ideas from metric domain theory have been later simplified and generalized to
categories of sheaves on frames with well-founded bases and the even simpler
case of presheaves on well-founded posets
[
6
]
, allowing for a

synthetic
and topos-theoretic approach to guarded domain theory
along the lines of synthetic domain theory
[
28
]
or synthetic
differential geometry
[
35
]
. The core idea of
synthetic
guarded domain theory
(
SGDT
) is to work with a topos
\mathscr{S}
that is equipped with an
endofunctor
\mathop{\blacktriangleright}
called the
later modality
, together with a natural
transformation

. Under mild assumptions (

.

), it can be seen
that
\mathop{\blacktriangleright}
extends to an endofunctor on the fundamental fibration

\mathbf{P}_{\mathscr{S}}
\mathscr{S}
and therefore gives rise to a true connective
in the internal dependent type theory of
\mathscr{S}
.

Synthetic guarded domain theory has been employed as a metalanguage for the
denotational
and
operational semantics of simple programming languages
such as PCF and
FPC
[
45
,
41
,
44
]
;
the models of
op. cit
.
can be seen as synthetic versions of Escardó’s
“analytic” metric model of PCF
[
24
]
. Synthetic guarded domain
theory also provides the mathematical basis
[
11
]
for
Iris
, a higher-order guarded
separation logic that has been used to develop operationally-based program
logics for sophisticated programming languages involving higher-order store,
concurrency, and a number of other computational effects
[
33
,
32
]
.

1.2
Multi-clock guarded recursion and coinduction

Unlike both classical and ordinary synthetic domain theory, the synthetic
guarded domain theory is
effective
in the sense that it gives rise to
type theories satisfying the
canonicity

property
[
26
]
; this means that synthetic guarded domain
theory is a
programming language
in addition to a semantic universe for
denotational semantics. One early application of guarded recursion in this
sense was to provide a more ergonomic and compositional method to write
programs involving coinductive types or
final coalgebras
.

Consider the type of infinite streams
\mathsf{S}A
as an example; this
type is the final coalgebra for the endofunctor
\mathsf{F}_{A}X=A\times X
, and
because
\mathsf{F}_{A}
is
\omega
-cocontinuous we may compute
\mathsf{S}A
as the
limit of the
\omega
-chain
\mathsf{F}_{A}^{n}1\cong A^{n}
by Adámek’s theorem. A
stream producer

by general recursion; such a
programming style is easily supported in languages like Haskell. But what is
the appropriate linguistic construct for defining
total
functions

\lxSVG@closescope {{

ill-defined.

One method to ensure that recursive functions on coinductive types are total is
to impose a
syntactic guardedness
check: every recursive call must be
wrapped in a call to a constructor. Syntactic guardedness checks are employed
in several type theoretic languages, such as Agda
[
43
]
,
Coq
[
22
]
, and Idris
[
15
,
16
]
, but
they are unfortunately very brittle and not at all conducive to compositional
and modular programming with higher-order functions. Type-based approaches such
as
sized types
have been proposed as a more compositional alternative
to syntactic checks
[
27
]
, but the meaning of sized
types as they are used in practice remains poorly understood — for instance,
the version of sized types implemented in Agda is clearly inconsistent as it
asserts the well-foundedness of an order with
\infty\prec\infty
; yet it
remains unclear whether many sized Agda programs would survive the transition
to a system in which
\infty\not\prec\infty
.
1
1

1

See

https://github.com/agda/agda/issues/2820
for a discussion of the
inconsistency of Agda’s sized types.

A
sound
and thus more promising type-based approach to ensuring the
guardedness of recursive calls arises from the later modality
\mathop{\blacktriangleright}
, first viewed as a programming construct by
Nakano
[
42
]
.
2
2

2

The calculus of Nakano
[
42
]
was subsequently connected to metric domain theory by Birkedal,
Schwinghammer, and Støvring
[
7
]
.
The
idea is to approximate the coinductive type
\mathsf{S}A\cong A\times\mathsf{S}A

by the
guarded recursive
type
\mathsf{S}_{\mathop{\blacktriangleright}}A\cong A\times\mathop{\blacktriangleright}{\mathsf{S}_{\mathop{\blacktriangleright}}A}
:

\mathsf{S}_{\mathop{\blacktriangleright}}:\mathsf{Type}\to\mathsf{Type}\qquad\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\dblcolon$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\dblcolon$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\dblcolon$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\dblcolon$}\mathclose{}{)}}:A\to\mathop{\blacktriangleright}\mathsf{S}_{\mathop{\blacktriangleright}}A\to\mathsf{S}_{\mathop{\blacktriangleright}}A

The guarded fixed point operator then allows recursive definitions of functions
on guarded streams, with the caveat that recursive calls must appear underneath
the later modality. While this semantic / type-based restriction does
automatically ensure totality, it is too conservative: we cannot, for instance,
define the projection functions
\mathsf{S}_{\mathop{\blacktriangleright}}A\to A^{n}
. For instance, the
following attempted definition is not well-typed:

\begin{array}[t]{ll}\mathsf{take}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathsf{S}_{\mathop{\blacktriangleright}}A\to\mathsf{List}\;A\\
\mathsf{take}\;0\;u=[]\\
\mathsf{take}\;(n+1)\;(x\dblcolon u)=x\dblcolon{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{take}\;n\;u}\end{array}

1.2.1
Atkey and McBride’s clock-indexed guarded recursion

At the heart of the problem discussed above is the fact that the guarded
streams only approximate the coinductive streams. The remarkable suggestion of
Atkey and McBride
[
3
]
is to
define
real
coinductive types in terms of their guarded approximations by adding an
additional notion of
clock
to the language; with this combination of
features, arbitrary functions on coinductive types can be defined using guarded
recursion. In the setting of Atkey and McBride, the later modality ensures that
functions are well-defined and the clocks allow the later modality to be

removed
in a type-constrained way. The language of Atkey and McBride
contains a new sort of clocks
k
, together with a clock-indexed family of
later modalities
\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{k}
as well as a
clock quantifier

\forall k.A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}
. In the case where
A
does not depend on the clock variable
k
, a

clock irrelevance
principle is asserted stating that
A=\forall k.A
; finally the canonical map
\lambda x.\Lambda k.\mathsf{next}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\hbox to160.44pt{\vbox to10pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 33.12158pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-33.12158pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{{{{}{}{}}}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{50.03821pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 48.6 3.46 L 65.78 3.46}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{47.78821pt}{2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.04 3.57 C -2.49 1.43 -1.25 0.42 0 0 C -1.25 -0.42 -2.49 -1.43 -3.04 -3.57}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
is
asserted to have an inverse
\mathsf{force}
.

A clock can be thought of metaphorically as a “time stream”; thus an element
of
\forall k.\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{k}A
exhibits an element of
\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{k}A
in all time streams

k
; thus under this metaphor, the
\mathsf{force}
operation simply instantiates
this family at an
earlier
time stream to obtain an element of
A
. With
the clock-indexed later modality in hand, it is now possible to define
coinductive streams in terms of their guarded approximations by setting

\mathsf{S}A\coloneqq\forall k.\mathsf{S}_{\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{k}}A
; thus we may use guarded
recursion to define the
\mathsf{take}
function on coinductive streams:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{ll}\mathsf{S}_{\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{k}}:\mathsf{Type}\to\mathsf{Type}\\
(\dblcolon):A\to\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{k}\mathsf{S}_{\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{k}}A\to\mathsf{S}_{\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{k}}A\\
\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{ll}\mathsf{uncons}_{k}:\mathsf{S}_{\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{k}}A\to A\times(\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{k}\mathsf{S}_{\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{k}}A)\\
\mathsf{uncons}_{k}\;(x\dblcolon u)=(x,u)\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{ll}\mathsf{head}:\mathsf{S}A\to A\\
\mathsf{head}\;u=\Lambda k.\mathsf{fst}\;\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{uncons}_{k}\;u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{uncons}_{k}\;u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{uncons}_{k}\;u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{uncons}_{k}\;u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\end{array}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{ll}\mathsf{tail}:\mathsf{S}A\to\mathsf{S}A\\
\mathsf{tail}\;u=\mathsf{force}(\Lambda k.\mathsf{snd}\;\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{uncons}_{k}\;u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{uncons}_{k}\;u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{uncons}_{k}\;u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{uncons}_{k}\;u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}})\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{ll}\mathsf{take}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathsf{S}A\to\mathsf{List}\;A\\
\mathsf{take}\;0\;u=[]\\
\mathsf{take}\;(\mathsf{suc}\;n)\;u=(\mathsf{head}\;u)\dblcolon(\mathsf{take}\;n\;(\mathsf{tail}\;u))\end{array}

1.2.2
Bizjak and Møgelberg’s clock synchronization; Sterling and Harper’s variant

One aspect of Atkey and McBride’s clocks that has proved difficult to account
for in a well-behaved way is that the substitution of clock variables is
restricted to avoid identifying “time streams” within types: to be precise,
arbitrary substitutions
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k^{\prime}/k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k^{\prime}/k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k^{\prime}/k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k^{\prime}/k$}\mathclose{}{]}}
are permitted in terms, but a substitution
in a
type
is only permitted when it does not cause two distinct clocks
to be identified. To implement this restriction, a somewhat bizarre side
condition on the instantiation rule for
\forall k.A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}
is needed, which
unfortunately appears to preclude the generalization of Atkey and McBride’s clocks to the
dependently typed setting,
pace
a worthy attempt by
Møgelberg
[
40
]
which was thwarted by the failure of the
substitution lemma.

Bizjak and Møgelberg
[
13
]
subsequently resolved
these difficulties in 2015 by simply removing the restriction on clock
substitution entirely: to substantiate this simplified language, they construct
a
fibered
presheaf model that supports
diagonal substitutions
of clocks, which
they refer to as
clock synchronization
. With synchronization in
place, there were two remaining problems left unresolved by
op. cit
.
:

(1)

the fibered character of the model caused coherence problems that impede the
interpretation of the syntax of dependent type theory;

(2)

also missing was the
clock irrelevance
principle, which
should at a minimum ensure that the canonical map
A
\forall k.A
is
an isomorphism for any type
A
that doesn’t depend on
k
.

A solution to the coherence problem was found in 2017 by Sterling and
Harper
[
52
]
, who employed the Grothendieck
construction to replace the fibered presheaf model by an equivalent

ordinary
presheaf model. Sterling and Harper also addressed clock
irrelevance in two steps: first, they interpreted the clock quantifier
\forall k
as an
intersection type
in an internal realizability
model;
3
3

3

See also Bizjak and
Birkedal
[
10
]
for a different realizability
approach to multi-clock guarded recursion via generalized equilogical spaces.

then they ensured that this intersection is non-trivial by
forcing
the
proposition
\exists k.\top
in the ambient topos. Around the same time,
Bizjak and Møgelberg
[
14
]
returned to the clock
synchronization model with an analogous solution to the coherence problem and a
more general approach to clock irrelevance: the quantifier

\forall k
remains a dependent product, but they restrict the language to types that are

orthogonal
to the object of clock names. The solution of Bizjak and
Møgelberg is more broadly applicable than that of Sterling and Harper
because intersection need not be meaningful in an arbitrary category whereas
products have a very simple universal property.

1.3
Goals and structure of this paper

Most models of single-clock synthetic guarded domain theory are given by
presheaf topoi; the models of multi-clock synthetic guarded domain theory are
also taken in presheaves, but of a different kind than the single-clock
version. Thus the work of

[
3
,
13
,
14
,
52
]

on multi-clock guarded recurison raises two questions concerning the
relationship between the existing models of single-clock and multi-clock
guarded recursion:

(1)

Does the passage from single-clock to multi-clock topos models have a
universal property?

(2)

Can the multi-clock model be rephrased as a special
case of the single-clock model of SGDT?

In this paper we answer both questions positively. Each multi-clock topos can
be seen to be a
partial product
or
bagtopos

[
55
,
29
,
30
]
for a certain
cocartesian fibration of topoi applied at a given model of single-clock guarded
recursion as hinted by Sterling
[
51
, §2.2.6]
; moreover we show that the model of synthetic guarded domain theory
in the multi-clock setting is an instance of the single-clock model generalized to the

relative Grothendieck topos theory
over a given elementary topos

\mathscr{S}
. Thus we have contributed a completely modular toolkit for negotiating
the two orthogonal axes of variation in multi-clock synthetic guarded domain
theory: the properties of the object of clocks, and the properties of each
later modality
\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{k}
.

Structure of this paper

In
Section

2
, we introduce the topos and category theory that
is needed for our technical development. As
relative
topos theory plays
an important role in our work, we
pay special attention to it. In
Section

3
, we define

elementary axioms
for both single-clock and multi-clock synthetic
guarded domain theory in a topos, and we contribute a toolkit for constructing
and transforming models of both.

(1)

In
Sections

3.3
and
3.4
we show that synthetic
guarded domain theory is stable under both presheaves and localization,
hence any bounded geometric morphism into a topos model of SGDT lifts this
model into its domain.

(2)

In
Section

3.5
we generalize the
results of Birkedal
et al
.

[
6
]
by constructing models of SGDT
in sheaves on frames with a well-founded basis
over an arbitrary base
topos
with a natural numbers object. This generalization requires a subtle change to the definition of
well-founded poset, as well as new constructive proofs of the existence of
term-level guarded fixed points.

In
Section

4
we provide an explicit description of the
geometric theories that extant presheaf models of single-clock guarded
recursion classify. In
Section

5
we give a general
construction of a multi-clock model from a single-clock model using the

bagtopoi
of Vickers
[
55
]
: in particular, a multi-clock
topos classifies the theory of a “bag” or “multi-set” of points of the
corresponding single-clock topos. Our characterization thus provides a
geometrical universal property for multi-clock guarded recursion as a model
construction on topoi,
and
as an explicit transformation of geometric
theories. Finally in
Section

6
, we use these
new universal properties to give a new and more abstract proof that the
multi-clock topoi are in fact models of synthetic guarded domain theory in each
clock.

2
Geometric universes, topoi, and logoi

Because the topic of the present paper is logico-geometric duality in guarded
domain theory, we are careful to distinguish the spatial aspects of topoi from
the logical ones in both our terminology and notations. Similar conventions are
employed by Vickers
[
56
]
, Bunge and Funk
[
21
]
,
and Anel and Joyal
[
2
]
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
geometric universe
4
4

4

Geometric universes in this sense are
usually referred to as
elementary topoi with natural numbers objects
.
is a cartesian closed
category
\mathscr{E}
that has finite limits, a subobject classifier
\Omega
, and a natural
numbers object
\mathbb{N}
.

We will write
\mathbf{Top}_{\mathscr{S}}
for the meta-2-category of
\mathscr{S}
-topoi for a
geometric universe
\mathscr{S}
. We will write
\mathbf{Log}_{\mathscr{S}}=\mathbf{Top}_{\mathscr{S}}^{\circ}
for the meta-2-category obtained by reversing the
1-cells but not the 2-cells; we refer to an object of
\mathbf{Log}_{\mathscr{S}}
as an

\mathscr{S}
-
logos
. Given a topos
X
, we may think of the dual logos

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{{\textbf{{X}}}}
as the category of
\mathscr{S}
-valued
sheaves
on the space

X
.
Indeed, the
relative
Giraud theorem states that the logos
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{{\textbf{{X}}}}

can be equivalently presented as a left exact localization of a category of
internal diagrams
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{C},\mathscr{S}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{C},\mathscr{S}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{C},\mathscr{S}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{C},\mathscr{S}$}\mathclose{}{]}}
for some internal category

\mathbb{C}
in
\mathscr{S}
.

2.1
Geometric theories and classifying topoi

The notion of
geometric theory
elucidates the relationship between topoi and logoi.

Each geometric theory
\mathbb{T}
determines a
classifying topos

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}
whose points form the category of
\mathbb{T}
-models and

\mathbb{T}
-model homomorphisms; the dual logos
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}}
is then the

classifying category
or category of contexts and substitutions for the
theory
\mathbb{T}
. It is also useful to think of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}}
as the
universal extension of the geometric universe
\mathscr{S}
by an indeterminate

\mathbb{T}
-model in the same way that the polynomial ring
A[x]
is the free
extension of a ring
A
by an indeterminate element.

There are several possible notions of geometric theory over
\mathscr{S}
; for the
sake of this paper, we choose a particularly syntactical one. Informally a
geometric theory over
\mathscr{S}
is given by a collection of sorts
\sigma
and predicates
\overline{x:\sigma}\mid\phi\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\vec{x}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\vec{x}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{x}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{x}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, together with a collection of sequents

type theory of
\mathscr{S}
.

Construction 2.4
.

The
free finite product completion

\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of any
internal
\mathscr{S}
-category
\mathbb{C}
can be computed explicitly in the
internal language of
\mathscr{S}
as follows:

(1)

an object
\Psi\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is a finite
\mathscr{S}
-cardinal
5
5

5

To be very
clear, by a finite cardinal we mean an element of the natural numbers object of
\mathscr{S}
;
a morphism of finite cardinals
m
n
is a function from
\mathbb{N}_{<m}
to

\mathbb{N}_{<n}
.

Example 2.5
.

The
theory of an object

\mathsf{ob}
over
\mathscr{S}
has a single sort
\mathsf{K}
, no
operations, and no axioms. To be more formal, the sorts of
\mathsf{ob}
are
parameterized by the terminal object
1_{\mathscr{S}}\in\mathscr{S}
and the
operations and axioms are parameterized by the initial object

\emptyset_{\mathscr{S}}\in\mathscr{S}
. Letting
\mathbb{O}=\mathbf{1}^{\times}
be the free internal
\mathscr{S}
-category
with finite products generated by a single object, then
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{ob}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{ob}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{ob}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{ob}$}\mathclose{}{]}}}

is the category of
\mathscr{S}
-valued presheaves on
\mathbb{O}
.

Example 2.6
.

The
theory of a pointed object

\mathsf{el}
over
\mathscr{S}
has a single sort
\mathsf{K}
,
a single constant
\mathsf{k}:\mathsf{K}
, and no axioms. Recalling
\mathbb{O}
from Example
2.5
, then

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{el}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{el}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{el}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{el}$}\mathclose{}{]}}}
is the category of
\mathscr{S}
-valued presheaves on

\mathbb{O}\downarrow\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle*$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle*$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle*$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
.

Definition 2.8

(Algebraic topos)
.

An
\mathscr{S}
-topos
X
is called
algebraic
when there exists an
internal category
\mathbb{C}\in\mathscr{S}
and an equivalence of
\mathscr{S}
-topoi

X
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
.

Thus algebraic morphisms are closed under composition, and moreover,
composition of algebraic morphisms corresponds to the
Grothendieck
construction
for the internal categories that determine them.

2.3.1
Slices and étale morphisms

Let
\mathscr{S}
be a geometric universe and fix an object
A\in\mathscr{S}
. It is
sometimes referred to as the “fundamental theorem of topos theory” that the
slice
\mathscr{S}\downarrow{}A
is again a geometric universe; moreover, the pullback functor

\boldsymbol{\mathscr{K}}
as the polynomial expression

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}\mkern-4.0mu_{p}{A}={\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum}{\textstyle\sum}{\scriptstyle\sum}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum}}_{b:B}{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\textstyle\prod}{\scriptstyle\prod}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod}}_{e:p\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle b$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle b$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle b$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle b$}\mathclose{}{]}}}A
.

3
Elementary synthetic guarded domain theory

In this section, we set down
elementary
axioms for synthetic guarded
domain theory in a geometric universe
\mathscr{S}
. In

Sections

3.3
and
3.4
we study the stability of these
axioms under the two fundamental topos-theoretic constructions: presheaves and
left exact localization. In
Section

3.5
we generalize the results of
Birkedal
et al
.
to construct a base model from a frame with a well-founded basis
over any base geometric universe.

3.1
Elementary axioms for synthetic guarded domain theory

Scholium 3.3
.

When the later modality has a left adjoint
{\blacktriangleleft}\dashv\mathop{\blacktriangleright}
,
the well-pointedness condition of
Definition

3.2
is equivalent to
the
tick irrelevance
property isolated by Mannaa, Møgelberg, and
Veltri
[
38
]
.

.

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
on the subobject classifier that preserves
finite conjunctions. Likewise, the later modality internalizes as a

fibered endofunctor
on the fundamental fibered category of
\mathscr{S}
as
in Birkedal
et al
.

[
6
]
, and can hence be used informally in the
internal dependent type theory of
\mathscr{S}
.

Definition 3.5
.

A later modality structure is said to support

Löb induction
when the sequent
\phi:\Omega\mid\mathop{\blacktriangleright}{\phi}\Rightarrow\phi\vdash\phi
holds in the internal logic of

\mathscr{S}
.

guarded recursive
terms

such that

.

The following result follows from the
unique choice
principle valid in any geometric universe.

Lemma 3.6
.

A well-pointed later modality structure supports guarded recursive terms if and only if it supports Löb induction.

Scholium 3.7
.

As a consequence of
Lemma

3.6
, it is rarely necessary to verify
the guarded recursive terms property, which is always more complex to check
than Löb induction. In fact,
Lemma

3.6
is an important
ingredient to our constructivization of the results of
Birkedal
et al
.

[
6
]
in
Section

3.5
: it allows us to
sidestep the very technical and non-constructive proof of Lemma 8.13 in
op. cit
.

We do not here consider algebraic compactness conditions on
\mathscr{S}
with
respect to contractive functors, although these usually play an important role
in the solution of domain equations in synthetic guarded domain
theory
[
6
]
. Instead we take the point of view of Birkedal and
Møgelberg
[
5
]
and advocate solving domain
equations internally to
\mathscr{S}
using term-level guarded recursion on universe
objects. Thus we adopt the following elementary
definition of a model of synthetic guarded domain theory.

Definition 3.8
.

An
elementary geometric model
of synthetic guarded domain theory is
given by a geometric universe
\mathscr{S}
equipped with a well-pointed later
modality
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
that supports Löb induction.

Every geometric universe
\mathscr{S}
carries a trivial model of synthetic guarded
domain theory where
\mathop{\blacktriangleright}{A}=1_{\mathscr{S}}
. It is therefore important to
distinguish non-trivial models in order to implement
adequate

denotational semantics; the following definition is one possible restraint on
the later modality:

.

\mathscr{S}
be a geometric universe equipped with a later modality structure

Theorem 3.11

(Stability under presheaves)
.

The pointwise later modality structure

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright}^{\mathbb{C}},\mathsf{next}^{\mathbb{C}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright}^{\mathbb{C}},\mathsf{next}^{\mathbb{C}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright}^{\mathbb{C}},\mathsf{next}^{\mathbb{C}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright}^{\mathbb{C}},\mathsf{next}^{\mathbb{C}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
on

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}
is well-pointed and supports Löb induction. Hence
the category of diagrams
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}
is an elementary geometric
model of synthetic guarded domain theory.

The following can be proved pictorially in the language of wiring diagrams; see
Section

A.2
for details.

Lemma 3.13
.

If
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a well-pointed later modality structure on
\mathscr{S}

and
L:\hbox to29.69pt{\vbox to7.16pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77779pt\lower-0.33063pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{L},\mathsf{next}_{L}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{L},\mathsf{next}_{L}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{L},\mathsf{next}_{L}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{L},\mathsf{next}_{L}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
defined in

3.12
is well-pointed.

Theorem

3.14
follows nearly immediately from an
internal logic argument, using the closure modality associated to any left
exact localization.

Theorem 3.14

(Stability under localization)
.

If the later modality structure
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
on
\mathscr{S}
supports Löb
induction, then so does
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{L},\mathsf{next}_{L}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{L},\mathsf{next}_{L}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{L},\mathsf{next}_{L}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{L},\mathsf{next}_{L}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
for a left exact localization

L:\hbox to29.69pt{\vbox to7.16pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77779pt\lower-0.33063pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

. Hence if
\mathscr{S}
is an elementary geometric model of
synthetic guarded domain theory, then so is any left exact localization of

\mathscr{S}
.

By the above, we may conclude that
any
category
\mathscr{E}
of

\mathscr{S}
-valued sheaves inherits an elementary model of synthetic guarded domain
theory from the base geometric universe
\mathscr{S}
, if it is so-equipped; note
this model could depend on the chosen presentation of
\mathscr{E}
by an
\mathscr{S}
-site.
Special care must be taken, as localizations need not preserve global adequacy;
for example, the localization could
trivialize
the later modality in
the sense of making
\mathop{\blacktriangleright}_{L}{A}=1_{\mathscr{T}}
for all
A\in\mathscr{T}
.

3.5
Base models from intuitionistic well-founded posets

In the preceeding sections we have shown stability of elementary geometric
models of synthetic guarded domain theory under basic topos theoretic
constructions: presheaves and localization; from these stability properties it
follows that the logos presented by
any

\mathscr{E}
-site inherits guarded
recursion from an elementary geometric model
\mathscr{E}
. But how do we construct a
geometric model
\mathscr{E}
in the first place? Birkedal
et al
.

[
6
]

provide a simple recipe for constructing such models, working in the more
restrictive setting of
\mathbf{Set}
-logoi; in particular, it is verified that for any

\mathbf{Set}
-locale
X
whose frame of opens
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{O}}\mkern-1.0mu_{{\textbf{{X}}}}
has a well-founded
basis, the logos
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{{\textbf{{X}}}}
is an elementary geometric model of synthetic
guarded domain theory.

In this section, we carry out a (non-trivial) generalization of the results of

op. cit
.
to
\mathscr{S}
-locales for an arbitrary geometric universe
\mathscr{S}
. Indeed,
not only are the arguments of
op. cit
.
non-constructive and thus invalid over an
arbitrary geometric universe: the definition of
well-founded poset
must
be adjusted as well:

(1)

It will not do to define
u<v
as
\lnot\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle v\leq u$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle v\leq u$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle v\leq u$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v\leq u$}\mathclose{}{)}}
unless
\mathscr{S}

is boolean; thus the well-founded order on a poset must be an additional
structure that is compatible with the original order in a certain way.

(2)

The classical definition of well-foundedness in terms of infinite descending
chains is correct if and only if
\mathscr{S}
satisfies dependent choice.

3.5.1
Basic definitions: intuitionistic well-founded posets and frames

Definition 3.15
.

Let
R\subseteq P\times P
be a binary relation in
\mathscr{S}
. The

R
-accessible elements
of
P
are the smallest subset

\mathsf{Acc}_{R}\subseteq P
spanned by elements
u\in P
such that for all

v\mathrel{R}u
we have
v\in\mathsf{Acc}_{R}
.
We say that
R
is
well-founded
when
\mathsf{Acc}_{R}\subseteq P
is
P
itself.

Definition 3.16
.

Let
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}
be a preorder in
\mathscr{S}
; we define a
compatible
well-founded relation
on
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}
to be a transitive binary subrelation
{\prec}\subseteq{\leq}\subseteq\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P}
satisfying the following additional axioms:

(1)

Left compatibility.
If
u\leq v
and
v\prec w
then
u\prec w
.

(2)

Right compatibility.
If
u\prec v
and
v\leq w
then
u\prec w
.

(3)

Well-foundedness.
The relation
{\prec}\subseteq\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P}
is well-founded.

Definition 3.17
.

We define an
intuitionistic well-founded preorder
to be a triple

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{P},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{P},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}
in
\mathscr{S}
such that
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a preorder and

{\prec}
is a compatible well-founded relation on
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Likewise
we will speak of an
intuitionistic well-founded poset
to refer to an
intuitionistic well-founded preorder for which
\leq
satisfies
anti-symmetry.

Definition 3.18

(Connected poset)
.

Let
\mathbb{P}
poset object in
\mathscr{S}
. We say that
u,v\in\mathbb{P}
are
comparable
if

u\leq v
or
v\leq u
. A poset object is
connected
if for each
u,v\in\mathbb{P}
there
exists a finite sequence
u=c_{0},\dots,c_{n}=v
in
\mathbb{P}
such that
c_{i}
and
c_{i+1}

are comparable for each
i
.

Definition 3.19

(Internal frames)
.

A
frame
in
\mathscr{S}
is defined to be a poset object that is closed
under
\mathscr{S}
-joins and finite meets, such that finite meets distribute over

\mathscr{S}
-joins.

Definition 3.20

(Basis for a frame)
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a frame in
\mathscr{S}
and let
\mathbb{K}\subseteq\mathbb{A}
be a subposet of
A
;
then
\mathbb{K}
is called a
basis
for
\mathbb{A}
when every
u\in\mathbb{A}
is the least
upper bound of all the
k\in\mathbb{K}
such that
k\leq u
.

A base model in sheaves on a frame with well-founded basis

Any frame
\mathbb{A}
in
\mathscr{S}
gives rise to an internal site; the underlying
internal category is
\mathbb{A}
itself, and a family
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{v_{i}}\leq{u}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle{v_{i}}\leq{u}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v_{i}}\leq{u}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{v_{i}}\leq{u}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}

such that the direct image
i_{*}
regards
a sheaf as a presheaf and the inverse image

We first construct a later modality structure on the presheaf logos

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\widehat{\mathbb{A}}}=\mathbf{Pr}_{\mathscr{S}}{\mathbb{A}}=\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{A}^{\circ},\mathscr{S}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{A}^{\circ},\mathscr{S}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{A}^{\circ},\mathscr{S}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{A}^{\circ},\mathscr{S}$}\mathclose{}{]}}
assuming that there
exists an intuitionistic well-founded preorder
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{K},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{K},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{K},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{K},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}
that forms
a basis for
\mathbb{A}
. For each
u\in\mathbb{A}
, we will write
\mathbb{K}_{\leq u}\subseteq\mathbb{K}

for the subposet spanned by
k\in\mathbb{K}
such that
k\leq u
and
\mathbb{K}_{\prec u}

for the subposet spanned by
k\in\mathbb{K}
such that there exists
l\in\mathbb{K}_{\leq u}
with
k\prec_{\mathbb{K}}l
.
Following Birkedal
et al
.

[
6
]
we may define a
predecessor

operation on
\mathbb{A}
as a monotone endofunction:

\begin{array}[t]{ll}\mathsf{p}:\hbox to31.92pt{\vbox to7.16pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.75pt\lower-0.33063pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

. The following

Theorem 3.22

(Base model)
.

The well-pointed later modality structure
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\blacktriangleright},\mathsf{next}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
on

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}}
supports Löb induction, hence
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}}
is
an elementary geometric model of synthetic guarded domain theory.

4
Classifying topoi in single-clock guarded recursion

As Birkedal
et al
.

[
6
]
have pointed out, presheaves on a
well-founded poset are an instance of the sheaf models of guarded recursion
considered in
Section

3.5
. This is so because presheaves on a
well-founded poset
\mathbb{P}
are the same as sheaves on the
algebraic locale

\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
, whose frame of opens
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{O}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}}

is the free cocompletion of
\mathbb{P}
under all
\mathscr{S}
-joins and whose poset of points
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathds{1},\widehat{\mathbb{P}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathds{1},\widehat{\mathbb{P}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathds{1},\widehat{\mathbb{P}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathds{1},\widehat{\mathbb{P}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}
is the free
filtered
cocompletion of
\mathbb{P}^{\circ}
. Thus
\mathbb{P}
will turn out to be a
(well-founded) basis for
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{O}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}}
.

Explication.
Let
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{P},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{P},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}
be an intuitionistic well-founded
poset in
\mathscr{S}
; we may define an
\mathscr{S}
-locale
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
whose frame of
opens
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{O}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}}
consists of all the downsets of
\mathbb{P}
ordered by
inclusion,
i.e
.
the
\mathscr{S}
-poset of
\mathscr{S}
-poset homomorphisms

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\circ},\Omega$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{P}^{\circ},\Omega$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}^{\circ},\Omega$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{P}^{\circ},\Omega$}\mathclose{}{]}}
.
9
9

9

The frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{O}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}}
was
referred to by Birkedal
et al
.

[
6
]
as the
ideal completion

of
\mathbb{P}
. When
\mathbb{P}
is a total order,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{O}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}}
is indeed the
ideal completion of
\mathbb{P}
but this need not be the case otherwise; on the other
hand, the poset of points of the locale
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
under the specialization
order is indeed the ideal completion of
\mathbb{P}^{\circ}
by Diaconescu’s
theorem
[
23
]
.
Then the Yoneda embedding

\mathbb{P}
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{O}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}}
exhibits
\mathbb{P}
as a basis for
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{O}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}}
,
hence by
Theorem

3.22
we have an elementary geometric model of
synthetic guarded domain theory in
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}}
. Furthermore, the geometric
universe
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}}
can be seen to be the category
\mathbf{Pr}_{\mathscr{S}}{\mathbb{P}}
of

\mathscr{S}
-valued presheaves on the internal site
\mathbb{P}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{P},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{P},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq,\prec$}\mathclose{}{)}}
be an intuitionistic well-founded poset in

\mathscr{S}
. We will write
\mathbb{P}_{\prec}=\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle u$}\mathrel{{|}}\hbox{$\displaystyle\exists v.u\prec v$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle u$}\mathrel{{|}}\hbox{$\textstyle\exists v.u\prec v$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle u$}\mathrel{{|}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\exists v.u\prec v$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle u$}\mathrel{{|}}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\exists v.u\prec v$}\mathclose{}{\}}}

for the subposet spanned by elements lying strictly below another element. If
both
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\prec}
are connected, then the later
modality structure on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}}
is globally adequate relative
to
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}:\hbox to33.53pt{\vbox to10.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77779pt\lower-3.77777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.77779pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{{{{}{}{}}}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{19.69441pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.61 3.46 L 23.79 3.46}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{17.44441pt}{2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.04 3.57 C -2.49 1.43 -1.25 0.42 0 0 C -1.25 -0.42 -2.49 -1.43 -3.04 -3.57}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
.

Proof.

We note that
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}}\mathop{\blacktriangleright}{\mathbb{N}}
may be computed as
the limit
{\mathchoice{\textstyle\varprojlim}{\textstyle\varprojlim}{\scriptstyle\varprojlim}{\scriptscriptstyle\varprojlim}}_{u\in\mathbb{P}}{\mathchoice{\textstyle\varprojlim}{\textstyle\varprojlim}{\scriptstyle\varprojlim}{\scriptscriptstyle\varprojlim}}_{v\prec u}\mathbb{N}
, which is also the
connected limit
{\mathchoice{\textstyle\varprojlim}{\textstyle\varprojlim}{\scriptstyle\varprojlim}{\scriptscriptstyle\varprojlim}}_{u\in\mathbb{P}_{\prec}}\mathbb{N}
; hence

\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}}\mathop{\blacktriangleright}{\mathbb{N}}\cong\mathbb{N}\cong{\mathchoice{\textstyle\varprojlim}{\textstyle\varprojlim}{\scriptstyle\varprojlim}{\scriptscriptstyle\varprojlim}}_{u\in\mathbb{P}}\mathbb{N}\cong\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}}\mathbb{N}
.
∎

Most models of guarded recursion used in practice are indeed of this kind; in
addition to the global adequacy result (
Lemma

4.1
) and the
simplicity of working with presheaves, another advantage of the presheaf models
is that they can be characterized as classifying topoi for remarkably simple
and elegant geometric theories.

Lemma 4.2
.

For any poset
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{P},\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}
in
\mathscr{S}
, the
algebraic
\mathscr{S}
-topos
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}

classifies the geometric theory of
filters
on
\mathbb{P}
,
i.e
.
the theory
\mathsf{filter}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{P}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{P}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{P}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
axiomatized below:

\frac{u\in\mathbb{P}}{\cdot\vdash\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}:\mathsf{prop}}\and\frac{u\leq v\in\mathbb{P}}{\cdot\mid\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\vdash\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle v$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle v$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle v$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}\and\frac{}{\cdot\mid\top\vdash{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigvee}{\textstyle\bigvee}{\scriptstyle\bigvee}{\scriptscriptstyle\bigvee}}_{u\in\mathbb{P}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}\and\frac{u,v\in\mathbb{P}}{\cdot\mid\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle u$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\land\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle v$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle v$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle v$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\vdash{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigvee}{\textstyle\bigvee}{\scriptstyle\bigvee}{\scriptscriptstyle\bigvee}}_{w\in\mathbb{P},w\leq u,w\leq v}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle w$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle w$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle w$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle w$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}

Proof.

By Diaconescu’s theorem
[
23
]
, the topos
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}

classifies the theory of (internally)
\mathscr{S}
-valued flat functors on

\mathbb{P}
. A flat functor a poset is the same as a filter on that poset.
∎

Example 4.3

(The topos of trees)
.

The
topos of trees

[
6
]
over a geometric universe
\mathscr{S}

is defined to be the algebraic topos
\widehat{\omega}
where
\omega
is the
natural numbers object of
\mathscr{S}
with its usual order; this is the
“standard” model of synthetic guarded domain theory. We recall that
\widehat{\omega}=\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{filter}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{filter}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{filter}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{filter}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}
from
Lemma

4.2
; because

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\omega,\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\omega,\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\omega,\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\omega,\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a total
order in
\mathscr{S}
, the downward-directedness axiom for filters can be dropped
and we see that
\widehat{\omega}
classifies models of the following even simpler geometric theory:

\frac{n:\omega}{\cdot\mid\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle n$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle n$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle n$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle n$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}:\mathsf{prop}}\and\frac{m\leq n:\omega}{\cdot\mid\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\vdash\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle n$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle n$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle n$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle n$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}\and\frac{}{\cdot\mid\top\vdash{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigvee}{\textstyle\bigvee}{\scriptstyle\bigvee}{\scriptscriptstyle\bigvee}}_{i\in\omega}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle i$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}

Example 4.4

(Successor ordinals)
.

We may consider the successor
\omega^{+}=\omega\star\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\infty$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\infty$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\infty$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
that
adjoins a terminal element to
\omega
. The algebraic topos
\widehat{\omega^{+}}

classifies a geometric theory analogous to that of

Example

4.3
, but it can also be seen to classify a simpler

cartesian
theory by virtue of the fact that
\omega^{+}
has all finite
meets:

\frac{\alpha:\omega^{+}}{\cdot\mid\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}:\mathsf{prop}}\and\frac{\alpha\leq\beta:\omega^{+}}{\cdot\mid\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\vdash\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\beta$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\beta$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\beta$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}\and\frac{}{\cdot\mid\top\vdash\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\infty$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\infty$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\infty$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}

The order above is adapted from Taylor

on

. By

, and thus the classifying topos of filters on this poset carries
a model of SGDT. It is not difficult to see by unrolling definitions that

.

5
The universal property of multi-clock guarded recursion

5.1
Bagtopoi as partial products

Given a geometric theory
\mathbb{T}
over
\mathscr{S}
, there exists a geometric theory

\mathsf{bag}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
over
\mathscr{S}
of families of
\mathbb{T}
-models indexed by an
object of
\mathscr{S}
. We will give an explicit description of
\mathsf{bag}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
for

\mathbb{T}
a propositional geometric theory (
i.e
.
a theory with no sorts and only
nullary predicates).

Definition 5.1

(Johnstone
[
29
]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a propositional geometric theory. Then
\mathsf{bag}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is the theory
with a single sort
\mathsf{K}
together with:

(1)

for every proposition symbol
\phi
of
\mathbb{T}
, a predicate
k:\mathsf{K}\mid\phi\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}:\mathsf{prop}
;

(2)

for every axiom
\cdot\mid\phi\vdash\psi
of
\mathbb{T}
, an axiom
k:\mathsf{K}\mid\phi\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}\vdash\psi\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}\mathclose{}{]}}
.

Definition 5.2

(Johnstone
[
31
, Proposition B4.4.16]
)
.

Let
E
be an
\mathscr{S}
-topos. Let
p:\hbox to115.76pt{\vbox to10pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 22.04446pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-22.04446pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{{{{}{}{}}}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{38.96109pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

{{{{{}}{

}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 33.27 3.46 L 45.58 3.46}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{33.06396pt}{2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.79 0 L 0.35 2.59 L 0 0 L 0.35 -2.59 Z}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
be
the generic étale morphism of
\mathscr{S}
-topoi projecting the underlying object from a
pointed object. The
(lower) bagtopos
over
E
is the partial product

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{L}}{{\textbf{{E}}}}:=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}\mkern-4.0mu_{p}{{\textbf{{E}}}}
.

Observation 5.3

(Johnstone
[
31
, Proposition B4.4.16]
)
.

For a geometric theory
\mathbb{T}
, the bagtopos
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{L}}{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}}

is the classifying
\mathscr{S}
-topos for the theory
\mathsf{bag}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
,
i.e
.
we have

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{L}}{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{]}}}\simeq\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{bag}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{bag}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{bag}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{bag}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}
.

5.2
A universal property for Bizjak and Møgelberg’s clocks

Let
\mathbb{K}
be the
\mathscr{S}
-category having as its objects pairs
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle U,f\in\omega^{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle U,f\in\omega^{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle U,f\in\omega^{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle U,f\in\omega^{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
with
U
a finite
\mathscr{S}
-cardinal, while morphisms
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle U,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle U,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle U,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle U,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle V,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle V,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle V,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle V,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}
are given by functions
h:U\to V
such that
h;g\leq f\in\omega^{U}
in the pointwise ordering. The internal category
\mathbb{K}
above is exactly
the
category of time objects
written
\mathbb{T}
by Bizjak and
Møgelberg
[
14
]
, relativized over an arbitrary
geometric universe. It was left unmentioned by
op. cit
.
that
\mathbb{K}
is the
free finite coproduct completion of
\omega^{\circ}
, an observation that sets
the stage for our present results.

We observe that
\mathbb{K}^{\circ}
is the free finite product completion
\omega^{\times}
of
\omega
; thus we define the

Bizjak–Møgelberg topos
over
\mathscr{S}
to be the algebraic
\mathscr{S}
-topos

{\textbf{{BM}}}\coloneqq\widehat{\omega^{\times}}
whose total geometric universe is

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{{\textbf{{BM}}}}=\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{K},\mathscr{S}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{K},\mathscr{S}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{K},\mathscr{S}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{K},\mathscr{S}$}\mathclose{}{]}}
.

Lemma 5.4

(Johnstone
[
31
, Example B4.4.17]
)
.

If
E
is the algebraic
\mathscr{S}
-topos presented by an
\mathscr{S}
-category

\mathbb{C}
, then the bagtopos
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{L}}{{\textbf{{E}}}}
admits an algebraic
presentation by
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
; in other words, we have

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{L}}{\widehat{\mathbb{C}}}\simeq\widehat{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}
.

Corollary 5.5

(Universal property)
.

The Bizjak–Møgelberg topos
BM
is equivalent to the bagtopos

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{L}}{\widehat{\omega}}
, hence
BM
classifies the geometric theory

\mathsf{bag}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{filter}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{filter}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{filter}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{filter}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. In other words, the category of morphisms of
topoi
X
BM
is exactly the category of pairs
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle K,\phi$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle K,\phi$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle K,\phi$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle K,\phi$}\mathclose{}{)}}

where
K\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{{\textbf{{X}}}}
and
\phi\subseteq K\times\omega
is an
K
-indexed family of filters on

\omega
internal to
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{{\textbf{{X}}}}
.

5.3
A universal property for Sterling and Harper’s clocks

Let
🕒
be the
\mathscr{S}
-category having as its objects pairs
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle U,f\in\omega^{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle U,f\in\omega^{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle U,f\in\omega^{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle U,f\in\omega^{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
with
U
a finite, nonzero
\mathscr{S}
-cardinal, while morphisms

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle U,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle U,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle U,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle U,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle V,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle V,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle V,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle V,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}
are given by functions
h:U\to V
such that

h;f\leq g\in\omega^{U}
in the pointwise ordering. Observe how
🕒
is exactly
the category of clocks described by Sterling and Harper
[
52
]
,
relativized over an arbitrary geometric universe.

Remark 5.6

(Image factorization
[
2
, 3.2.11–12]
)
.

Each morphism of topoi
f:\hbox to31.58pt{\vbox to7.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.66669pt\lower-0.33063pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

Example 5.7
.

The
theory of an inhabited object

\mathsf{inh}
over
\mathscr{S}
has a single
sort
\mathsf{K}
together with the axiom
\vdash\exists k:\mathsf{K}.\top
.
Let
\mathbb{C}
be an internal
\mathscr{S}
-category, and let
\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{\textit{inh}}

be the full subcategory of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
whose objects are nonzero cardinals.
Then
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\mkern-1.0mu_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{inh}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{inh}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{inh}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{inh}$}\mathclose{}{]}}}
is the category of
\mathscr{S}
-valued presheaves on
\mathbf{1}^{\times}_{\textit{inh}}
.

.

\mathbb{T}
be a geometric theory. Then
\mathsf{bag}_{\textit{inh}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
